AD-C1 · −1		60-sns.tex:11 (live), character 1,506, 17 Nov, 1:11 pm. Message 17 Nov, 1:11 pm; 60-sns.tex:7Changing muscle attachments, joint motion, inertia, or actuator behavior changes the mechanical response to a command and the sensory signals returned to the circuit. Lacking context.
A neuromechanical simulation couples a neural controller to a mechanical model of the body it controls. Motor-neuron activity recruits actuators, the resulting forces mov…
A neuromechanical simulation couples a neural controller to a mechanical model of the body it controls. Motor-neuron activity recruits actuators, the resulting forces move or load the joints, and sensory pathways carry measurements of that response back to the controller. I wanted to transfer a locomotor circuit from one model of the legs to another. A circuit that alternates the legs in suspension must also coordinate muscle force with foot contact if the model is to support weight. I developed tools to inspect that loop one connection at a time and to repeat a mechanical test after changing a connection.
Clarification. Issue 1. Define the loop before the historical transfer and explain why the fall prompted controlled tests.Answered locally and verified on reread; live thread awaits Ben.

Independent finding
IR01		60-sns.tex:11 (pre-edit live source); Ch 6 live 7, 13, 29, and 31; Ch 7 63 Revised 60-sns.tex:7.
A neural controller acts through the mechanics to which it is connected. Changing muscle attachments, joint motion, inertia, or actuator behavior changes the mechanical r…
A neural controller acts through the mechanics to which it is connected. Changing muscle attachments, joint motion, inertia, or actuator behavior changes the mechanical response to a command and the sensory signals returned to the circuit. A controller that produces alternating motor activity can therefore fail to coordinate loading when transferred to a different body. Investigating that transfer requires tools that make the mechanical assumptions, neural computations, and exchanged signals accessible to inspection and controlled testing.
Describe neural and mechanical models, actuator commands, and returning sensory measurements before the Li/Deng transfer. Credit the course project to Morrow and Bolen and the passive toe change to Bolen.The failure on ground contact matters once the reader understands what was connected and why a command can have a different mechanical consequence.Applied locally; see the independent reread.
Independent finding
IR02		60-sns.tex:67 (pre-edit live source); Ch 6 live 31, 37, 46, 67–81, 98, 107, and 111 Revised 60-sns.tex:71.
The developed capabilities serve different parts of one experiment (Table~\ref{tab:sns_capability}). Graphical tools support circuit construction and inspection. Headless…
The developed capabilities serve different parts of one experiment (Table~\ref{tab:sns_capability}). Graphical tools support circuit construction and inspection. Headless execution runs a configured experiment without opening its graphical interface, allowing repeated trials and parameter searches. Saved model identities, parameters, and recordings connect these forms of work. A universal graphical application orchestrating every preparation and calibration operation remains a proposed extension; the reported evidence concerns the implemented components and named workflows.
Pair each authored block, adaptation, interface, or driver with its upstream source, user action, and actual test in the contribution table. Retain the division between CAD assembly, OpenSim conversion, and the runtime bridge.The inventory names software categories without identifying the component a researcher opens or the result it produced. LM-002		60-sns.tex:29 live · proposed inspection priority -2 · revised 60-sns.tex:7, 60-sns.tex:29, 60-sns.tex:146, 60-sns.tex:201
This experience exposed a practical gap between assembling a neuromechanical model and using it to investigate control. Manual tuning made it difficult to isolate a fault…
This experience exposed a practical gap between assembling a neuromechanical model and using it to investigate control. Manual tuning made it difficult to isolate a faulty pathway, distinguish a mechanical error from a neural error, or reproduce a useful condition. Graphical construction could make circuits easier to inspect, while scripted execution could make parameter searches and repeated experiments practical. Both required explicit command and sensory interfaces, checked model mechanics, and evaluation criteria appropriate to the task.
The fall has competing testable explanations. The experiment compares a specified intervention on a common clock. A neuromechanical simulation couples a neural controlle…
The fall has competing testable explanations. The experiment compares a specified intervention on a common clock. A neuromechanical simulation couples a neural controller to a mechanical model of the body it controls. Motor-neuron activity recruits actuators, the resulting forces move or load the joints, and sensory pathways carry measurements of that response back to the controller. I wanted to transfer a locomotor circuit from one model of the legs to another. A circuit that alternates the legs in suspension must also coordinate muscle force with foot contact if the model is to support weight. I developed tools to inspect that loop one connection at a time and to repeat a mechanical test after changing a connection.
The motivation names diagnostic difficulties. Name one operation the researcher gained, such as rerunning the same controller after a pathway deletion.Addressed locally.

LM-003		60-sns.tex:31 live · proposed inspection priority -2 · revised 60-sns.tex:31, 60-sns.tex:77, 60-sns.tex:98
This chapter addresses the fourth dissertation objective by developing tools for constructing and testing coupled neural and mechanical models. The methods connect OpenSi…
This chapter addresses the fourth dissertation objective by developing tools for constructing and testing coupled neural and mechanical models. The methods connect OpenSim- and CAD-derived mechanics, graphical neural construction, actuator and sensory interfaces, staged calibration, and automated experiments. The results proceed from mechanical and circuit checks to coupled responses, controlled interventions, and computational performance. OpenSim, MyoConverter, MuJoCo, SNS-Toolbox, Simulink, and Simscape provide the existing software foundations \citep{delp_opensim_2007,caggiano_myosuite_2022,todorov_mujoco_2012,nourse_sns_2023}. My contributions are the model preparation and repairs, editable blocks, actuator integrations, and experiment procedures that connect those foundations. Appendix~\ref{app:simulation_verification} gives supporting configurations, source scripts, and secondary comparisons.
Platforms and authored components are distinguished, with named scripts, operator actions, and test results. I built on OpenSim, MyoConverter, MuJoCo, SNS-Toolbox, Simul…
Platforms and authored components are distinguished, with named scripts, operator actions, and test results. I built on OpenSim, MyoConverter, MuJoCo, SNS-Toolbox, Simulink, and Simscape \citep{delp_opensim_2007,caggiano_myosuite_2022,todorov_mujoco_2012,nourse_sns_2023}. My contribution was to prepare mechanical models, implement editable neural and actuator blocks, connect neural outputs with actuator commands and mechanical measurements with sensory inputs, and write drivers that repeat interventions with a saved controller. The methods below describe how to assemble an experiment. The results start with a single joint, then examine how sensory return and mechanical assistance change bilateral coordination. Appendix~\ref{app:simulation_verification} gives the executable examples and scoring definitions.

The workflow begins with a mechanical model and a control hypothesis (Figure~\ref{fig:simulation_workflow}). Model preparation establishes the coordinates, actuator route…
The workflow begins with a mechanical model and a control hypothesis (Figure~\ref{fig:simulation_workflow}). Model preparation establishes the coordinates, actuator routes, force calculations, and contacts through which neural activity will act. Controller construction defines the computations performed by neural populations and the signals they exchange. Calibration selects parameters for those functions, and physical evaluation tests the resulting coupled behavior. Recordings then connect changes in the circuit to changes in movement and loading. A failed test can require revising an earlier component before repeating the coupled experiment.
The suggested first experiment follows an extensor sensory input to a measured motor response. To test a pathway, first choose a mechanical model and identify what its a…
The suggested first experiment follows an extensor sensory input to a measured motor response. To test a pathway, first choose a mechanical model and identify what its actuators can apply and its sensors can measure (Figure~\ref{fig:simulation_workflow}). Then specify the neural calculation and the physical condition under which its effect should be observable. For example, a sensory current applied to the extensor pathway of a loaded knee should change MN activity before it changes knee angle. A recording of both signals tests that sequence.
The general sequence can point directly to the first prepared model and signals on the drawing instead of listing abstract stages twice.Addressed locally.

The complete feedback loop connects MN output to activation or a pressure command, the actuator model to tensile force, and the mechanical state to sensory encoding. Length, lengthening velocity, and force provide inputs for Ia, II, and Ib pathways; foot contact supplies a distinct event or load signal. Their encoding ranges, signs, saturation, and update intervals must be stated. A synaptic conductance is not itself a sensory force, and a normalized neural output is not a pressure until the command mapping defines that conversion. Appendix~\ref{sec:app_signal_contracts} records these interface distinctions.
A concrete loop identifies antagonist actuation, knee motion, Ia currents, and tension-derived Ib currents. In the reduced knee, extensor and flexor MN outputs pass thro…
A concrete loop identifies antagonist actuation, knee motion, Ia currents, and tension-derived Ib currents. In the reduced knee, extensor and flexor MN outputs pass through activation dynamics to antagonist tensile actuators. Their moments change the knee angle and angular velocity. The spindle-like transducers use joint motion to produce Ia currents, and the Ib transducers use actuator tension. Each Ia pathway excites its own MN and inhibits the antagonist. Each Ib pathway inhibits its own MN. The resulting six connections close the loop that the worked example tests. The Gait2392 and W2L experiments use different sensory encoders and input ports, identified in Appendix~\ref{sec:app_signal_contracts}.

Functional design specifies what a neural pathway should compute before its parameters are fitted. For a PF channel, the target includes the part of the cycle during which the channel should drive its MNs, the duration of that drive, and its overlap with neighboring channels. The test therefore examines the activity transmitted through its synapse. Voltage below the synapse's operating range can vary without changing motor recruitment. LM-046		70-overall.tex:6 live · proposed inspection priority -1 · revised 70-overall.tex:6
The research program connects two goals: using biology to improve robots and using biomimetic robots to investigate biology. Actuator mechanics and neural control must be…
The research program connects two goals: using biology to improve robots and using biomimetic robots to investigate biology. Actuator mechanics and neural control must be considered together if a legged robot is to adapt to uneven terrain, disturbances, and changing loads. A sufficiently characterized robot also permits controlled changes to mechanics and sensory pathways that would be impractical or unethical in human experiments. Prosthetic and orthotic devices provide a further application of the resulting design and control knowledge.
The discussion opens with fixed-joint moment capacity and a loaded stepping task. Designing a robot knee for human-scale torque sets two different tests. With the joint …
The discussion opens with fixed-joint moment capacity and a loaded stepping task. Designing a robot knee for human-scale torque sets two different tests. With the joint fixed, the actuator and its route must transmit the required moment to the shank. During a step, the controller must recruit that actuator while the leg is in a position to accept or release load. A knee can meet the first requirement and still fail the second. The same distinction matters when using a robot to study biology. A change in foot loading after a sensory pathway is disabled has an interpretable cause only when the actuator, route, and external support are known.
The opening spans adaptation, biomedical uses, and research ethics in one paragraph. Lead with the interaction between actuator loading and feedback.Addressed locally.

This closes an actuator-sizing gap; it does not identify the complete dynamic behavior of a pressurized actuator.

The measured length effect and the proposed inter-batch comparison are separated. The force available from a Festo BPA depends on its resting length as well as its pressure and contraction. Chapter~\ref{ch:muscles} measured that length dependence and combined a maximum-force model with a three-coefficient nondimensional force surface. A designer can therefore compare candidate lengths at the operating pressure and contraction required by a joint, within the range characterized.

Dynamic characterization should distinguish active pressurized-braid force, passive resistance, and contraction dynamics. Biological muscle models provide a useful organi…
Dynamic characterization should distinguish active pressurized-braid force, passive resistance, and contraction dynamics. Biological muscle models provide a useful organizational framework for these components \citep{thelen_adjustment_2003,millard_flexing_2013}; Appendix~\ref{app:muscle_calcs} gives their constitutive relations. Constant-speed, constant-load, and constant-pressure experiments would extend the isometric measurements. The pressure--force model of \citet{elzein_pressure_2025} supplies a complementary starting point for pulse-modulated actuation.
Free, suspended-load, and fixed-separation tests identify measurements and the question each tests. One explanation for the resting-length effect concerns the shape of t…
Free, suspended-load, and fixed-separation tests identify measurements and the question each tests. One explanation for the resting-length effect concerns the shape of the pressurized tube. Near each end fitting the outer diameter remains close to its uninflated value. It increases through a transition region to the nominal inflated diameter in the central span (Figure~\ref{fig:bpa_construction}A). If the transition occupies a similar axial length at a given pressure, a shorter actuator has a smaller fraction of its wall operating as a uniform cylinder. The physical end cap raises a different question. Measuring relative displacement across Onyx and aluminum caps under matched tensile loads, with the actuator pressurized and unpressurized, would isolate the contribution of cap and attachment compliance. An axial profile of tube diameter is needed to test the transition-region explanation.

LM-053		70-overall.tex:49 live · proposed inspection priority -2 · revised 70-overall.tex:49
Controlled-speed and controlled-load knee tests would extend the static capacity measurements to movement. Loading--unloading sequences would characterize hysteresis, and…
Controlled-speed and controlled-load knee tests would extend the static capacity measurements to movement. Loading--unloading sequences would characterize hysteresis, and pressure-step tests would characterize the pneumatic response. A torque-controlled motor and a less compliant loading connection would reduce winch slack and permit these tests on the existing rig. These experiments would establish where the isometric model remains useful and where velocity dependence, damping, or pressure regulation needs an additional model.
The rig measures signed, synchronized torque and angular velocity and distinguishes energy delivery from absorption. Isometric tests establish torque capacity at zero an…
The rig measures signed, synchronized torque and angular velocity and distinguishes energy delivery from absorption. Isometric tests establish torque capacity at zero angular velocity. To determine when that capacity can do work, I would measure knee torque $\tau$ and angular velocity $\omega$ on the same time base and about the same signed axis. Their product $P=\tau\omega$, in watts, is the instantaneous mechanical power delivered by the knee to the external load. Positive power indicates energy delivery and negative power indicates absorption. Its timing and magnitude distinguish a knee that supplies energy during extension from one that resists motion to absorb a landing load. A biomechanist can compare these phases with joint-power profiles, and a robot designer can size the actuator for both propulsion and controlled deceleration. A dynamometer on the test stand could drive flexion and extension at prescribed speeds or impose a resisting torque while the BPA moves the knee. An encoder and a joint-axis torque transducer would supply synchronized velocity and torque. Recording actuator pressure and calibrating fixture inertia, friction, and loading-connection compliance would help distinguish pneumatic delay and load--unload hysteresis from losses in the rig.
State why synchronized dynamic torque and angular velocity matter, give P=τω and sign convention, and tie the dynamometer/loading proposal to delivering or absorbing power.Addressed locally.

LM-054		70-overall.tex:53 live · proposed inspection priority -2 · revised 70-overall.tex:53, 96-AppendixE.tex:20, 96-AppendixE.tex:49

Chapter~\ref{ch:database} addresses a different gap: selecting sensory feedback requires evidence distributed across animal studies, human studies, and computational models. SADb makes that evidence searchable through curation notes, linked records, classifications, and citation neighborhoods. It supports tracing a proposed pathway to its source and comparing the conditions under which a response was observed. The contribution is the research collection and its navigation tools, rather than a claim that every record is fully classified or every proposed feedback rule transfers directly to a robot.
The reader can find the McCrea record by the actual filter, follow its DOI, and distinguish the observed effects by phase and preparation. SADb lets a researcher start w…
The reader can find the McCrea record by the actual filter, follow its DOI, and distinguish the observed effects by phase and preparation. SADb lets a researcher start with a proposed sensory connection and inspect the experiment behind it. In the Table view, selecting \emph{Ib disynaptic excitation} under \emph{Pathway} returns linked papers. The record for \citet{mccrea_disynaptic_1995} opens a curation note, its pathway links, and the article DOI. In decerebrate cats, stimulating the plantaris nerve inhibited medial gastrocnemius motoneurons at rest. During the extensor phase of midbrain-evoked fictive locomotion, the same stimulation excited these synergist motoneurons. In an acute spinal preparation treated with clonidine, inhibition decreased without the short-latency excitation. The record also links to \emph{Ib disynaptic inhibition}. Reading the original experiment shows why those two links belong together and why a controller designer would test a state-dependent group~I pathway. The stimulation recruited group~I afferents and did not isolate Ib input.
Demonstrate the navigation claim with an actual pathway, paper, conditions, and an engineering inference. Remove the immediate unrelated qualification.Addressed locally.

In the proposed hardware implementation, Teensy-class microcontrollers would handle joint-level measurements and valve commands, and an NVIDIA Jetson Orin would evaluate the neural circuit. I would reconstruct length and velocity from encoders and the calibrated path geometry, and use measured tension for force feedback. Pressure transducers, foot contacts, and inertial measurements would complete the channels required by the selected controller. Table~\ref{tab:app_thelen_symbols} defines the curve parameters. A shape factor is a dimensionless parameter controlling a curve's curvature or width; it is not a stiffness in \unit{\newton\per\meter}. For example, increasing $\gamma$ broadens the active force--length curve while preserving its peak at $\bar L^M=1$. Changing $k^{PE}$ changes the passive curve's shape while retaining unit force at strain $\varepsilon_0^M$.

The human torque targets of Section~\ref{sec:optimization} are OpenSim tendon-excursion moment arms: for each muscle, the moment arm about the knee is the partial derivative of muscle-tendon length with respect to the knee coordinate, with positive tensile force and torque positive in the coordinate direction, $r(\theta_{k}) = -\partial l_{\mathrm{MT}} / \partial\theta_{k}$, evaluated across the range of motion. The exported analysis tables archived with the test data (\nolinkurl{OpenSim_Vasti_MomentArm.txt}, \nolinkurl{OpenSim_Vasti_Results.txt}, \nolinkurl{OpenSim_Bifem_MomentArm.txt}, \nolinkurl{OpenSim_Bifem_Results.txt}) carry the per-muscle moment arms and the corresponding maximum-isometric torque curves (moment arm multiplied by each muscle's maximum isometric force) for vastus intermedius, lateralis, and medialis, and for the biceps femoris short head; the optimization loads the vastus medialis and biceps femoris short-head columns as its targets (\texttt{buildKneeExtContext20mm.m} lines 119--140; \nolinkurl{Knee_Flexor_Data_20mm.m} line 546). For an unconstrained planar rotation, this derivative definition and the geometric perpendicular-foot construction of Equation~(\ref{eq:app_perpfoot}) below are equivalent for a straight muscle path crossing a planar revolute joint; the constrained and moving-path-point geometry is evaluated by OpenSim rather than by substituting the robot-side straight-path construction.

LM-082		94-AppendixC.tex:4 live · proposed inspection priority -1 · revised 94-AppendixC.tex:4
This appendix documents the numeric geometry and compliance parameters behind the force and torque models of Chapters~\ref{ch:muscles} and~\ref{ch:torque} as they exist i…
This appendix documents the numeric geometry and compliance parameters behind the force and torque models of Chapters~\ref{ch:muscles} and~\ref{ch:torque} as they exist in the project's MATLAB code, with file names given so that any value can be traced to the code that produced it. Route and bracket coordinates are coded in meters and reported here in millimeters; stiffnesses are coded and reported in newtons per meter. The bracket reference points are interpretation choices for the 3D-printed (Onyx FDM) attachment hardware, set from CAD by me; moving a reference point changes the identified stiffness values, so the points are recorded here alongside the values they produced.

LM-094		94-AppendixC.tex:341 live · proposed inspection priority -3 · revised 94-AppendixC.tex:341, 94-AppendixC.tex:387
The route-elimination rule that schedules the wrapped extensor route is a route-topology state machine rather than a shortest-path solver: the fully flexed route carries …
The route-elimination rule that schedules the wrapped extensor route is a route-topology state machine rather than a shortest-path solver: the fully flexed route carries every candidate contact, and each contact is released at the first more-extended knee angle at which the straight chord between its surviving neighbors can replace it without entering the exclusion envelopes. This section states the calculation in generic terms, so that it applies to any wrapped actuator route on a rotating joint pair; the extensor route of Table~\ref{tab:app_ext_seed} is the worked example, and the release schedule the calculation produces is the one quoted there.
The initial and concluding statements both specify the geometric and sweep assumptions. I used an ordered set of candidate contacts to model the sagittal extensor route …
The initial and concluding statements both specify the geometric and sweep assumptions. I used an ordered set of candidate contacts to model the sagittal extensor route in Table~\ref{tab:app_ext_seed}. The route crosses once from the femur to the tibia. Starting in full flexion, the model releases each contact as the knee extends when the chord between its surviving neighbors satisfies the rotation and clearance conditions below. The calculation uses prescribed contact locations and exclusion envelopes for both bodies.
The implemented test uses planar geometry, one crossing segment, and a monotonic angle sweep. Restrict 'any wrapped actuator route' to those assumptions.Addressed locally.

LM-096		94-AppendixC.tex:379 live · proposed inspection priority -2 · revised 94-AppendixC.tex:379
At the first pose of the sweep, full flexion, the seed route is settled to a fixpoint: every contact whose two gates already open there is removed, and removals repeat un…
At the first pose of the sweep, full flexion, the seed route is settled to a fixpoint: every contact whose two gates already open there is removed, and removals repeat until no further gate opens, so that the sweep starts from a valid topology; a colinearity guard may additionally remove a seed contact that starts the sweep already aligned with its neighbors, and its tolerance is zero in the design of record, so every seed row of Table~\ref{tab:app_ext_seed} starts active. At every later pose at most one contact is released: if several rows open both gates in the same pose, the row with the largest elimination margin, the one whose rotation rule is most decisively open, is released first, and the remaining rows are retested at the next pose. The release angle of row $j$ is recorded as the first grid pose at which the row is inactive. The activation angle is read from the same record with the sweep reversed, as the last pose at which the row is active, which is the pose at which the contact re-engages when the route is driven from extension toward flexion.
The reverse pose is explicitly inferred from the saved forward active set. At full flexion the algorithm repeatedly removes contacts for which both conditions are met, u…
The reverse pose is explicitly inferred from the saved forward active set. At full flexion the algorithm repeatedly removes contacts for which both conditions are met, until the remaining route satisfies the starting conditions. A collinearity check can also remove an already aligned contact. Its tolerance was zero for the reported design, and every seed row in Table~\ref{tab:app_ext_seed} started active. Each subsequent pose releases at most one contact. If several qualify, the algorithm selects the one with the largest elimination margin and retests the others at the next pose. The release angle is the first grid angle at which the contact is inactive. Reading the same saved sequence in reverse gives an inferred re-engagement angle, the adjacent grid pose at which the contact is active again.
Differentiate the first inactive forward grid pose and the inferred reverse activation angle; the reverse traversal of a stored record is not a measured re-engagement.Addressed locally.
Classifier inspection

is the bend signal handed to the force model. Each family's turn angle $\alpha_{f}$ ramps with knee angle between the two route-state events that bound it, holding zero b…
is the bend signal handed to the force model. Each family's turn angle $\alpha_{f}$ ramps with knee angle between the two route-state events that bound it, holding zero before the first event and its plateau after the second, so the topology state and the bending signal are two readings of one record. The formulation is joint-agnostic: it requires only a fixed route representation with one crossing segment, per-angle transforms between the two body frames, and clearance envelopes on each side, so it carries unchanged to the other wrapped routes of this program.
Any application to a second route requires one crossing, ordered sagittal contacts, supplied geometry, and monotonically releasing contacts. where each contribution is a…
Any application to a second route requires one crossing, ordered sagittal contacts, supplied geometry, and monotonically releasing contacts. where each contribution is a length at the specified knee angle and the integral spans the active condyle arc. Each family's turn angle ramps between the route-state events that bound it and reaches a plateau at full engagement. Applying the same release rule to another route requires an ordered set of sagittal contacts, one segment crossing between the two bodies, body transforms and clearance envelopes at every pose, and a sweep in which contacts release monotonically.
A planar, monotone one-crossing algorithm does not transfer unchanged to arbitrary 3-D or multi-joint routes.Addressed locally.

RR05		60-sns.tex:13We adjusted currents, synaptic weights, and muscle parameters by hand and presented the suspended model at the NIH BRAIN Initiative Investigators Meeting
For a course project in neuromechanical modeling, Morrow and I coupled the sagittal-plane biped of \citet{li_bipedal_2017} to the two-level central pattern generator (CPG…
For a course project in neuromechanical modeling, Morrow and I coupled the sagittal-plane biped of \citet{li_bipedal_2017} to the two-level central pattern generator (CPG) of \citet{deng_neuromechanical_2019} in AnimatLab \citep{cofer_animatlab_2010}. A shared rhythm generator (RG) drove pattern-formation (PF) pathways and motor neurons (MNs) on both sides. Antagonist Hill-type muscles acted on the thighs, shanks, and feet. I added a passive toe joint. We adjusted currents, synaptic weights, and muscle parameters by hand. Hunt and I reported the suspended model in a poster for the NIH BRAIN Initiative Investigators Meeting \citep{bolenTorqueValuesArtificialKnee2021June15-17}. Its legs cycled in suspension and the model tipped when released toward the ground (Figure~\ref{fig:animatlab_model_architecture}).
The course project is credited to Morrow and Bolen, the individual toe addition to Bolen, and the publication to Hunt and Bolen. The revised text says they reported it in a poster, avoiding an unsupported identification of the physical presenter.applied locally

D-01		APPENDIX_D_EDITS.md · Pass through the appendix
4–52, 79–81 This appendix provides the configuration details and secondary tests supporting Chapter~\ref{ch:sns}. The evidence follows the construction of a neuromechani…
4–52, 79–81 This appendix provides the configuration details and secondary tests supporting Chapter~\ref{ch:sns}. The evidence follows the construction of a neuromechanical experiment: check the mechanical model and signal interfaces, verify individual neural implementations, calibrate and test a selected controller, and examine its responses to controlled interventions. Historical comparisons are identified separately from the revised controller's current results. Neuron equations and spiking calibration remain in Appendix~\ref{app:neuron_equations}. \section{Mechanical-Model and Interface Verification}\label{sec:app_conversion_repairs} The conversion workflow involved two distinct problems: importing the robot's artificial-muscle routing with human muscle parameters, and repairing the converted human reference used for controller development. The first combination failed muscle-equilibrium checks because the routed lengths were incompatible with the retained human parameters. The subsequent human-model repairs described below belong to that reference-model loading path; they are not evidence that the BPA-routed model reproduces every human muscle force. \subsection{Conversion Repairs and Mechanical Checks} \paragraph{Joint directions and coordinate conventions.} Single-actuator tests exposed reversed hip-flexion and hip-adduction actions. The corresponding hinge-axis directions were negated so that the muscle actions agreed with the adopted OpenSim conventions. The knee required a different treatment: its converted flexion-negative convention was retained. Rotating the knee without updating its coupled translational coordinates gives a misleading geometry comparison, so those coordinates must follow the prescribed coupling during a pose or actuator test. These are specific joint and path checks, rather than a change from a left-handed to a right-handed model. \paragraph{Small muscles and force scales.} The quadratus femoris, gemelli, and piriformis actuators were disabled on both sides. Their six entries remain in the 92-actuator model, with no force contribution. A separate check found eight muscle-actuator entries with an active maximum-force scale of only \qty{1}{\newton}. Their active-force scales were restored from the source-model values. Table~\ref{tab:app_force_scale_repairs} lists the corrected values; each applies separately to the left and right actuator. \begin{table}[htbp] \centering \caption{Active maximum-force scales restored in the converted human model.} \label{tab:app_force_scale_repairs} \begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}} \toprule Muscle & Converted scale (\unit{\newton}) & Restored scale (\unit{\newton}) \\ \midrule Erector spinae & 1 & 2500 \\ Internal oblique & 1 & 900 \\ External oblique & 1 & 900 \\ Extensor hallucis longus & 1 & 162 \\ \bottomrule \end{tabular} \end{table} These are source-model maximum-force parameters, not measured BPA capacities or the realized force at every pose. The passive-force scales already contained the source values. The converted length ranges for these entries remain a limitation, so restoring the active scale alone does not establish full force--length agreement. \paragraph{Patella-related paths and coupled joints.} The converted moving muscle-path points use joints coupled to knee angle. Their sampled limits conflicted with the coupling constraints away from the sampled pose, producing large constraint forces and restricted motion. The coupled path-point joints were therefore left without those conflicting limits while retaining their prescribed trajectories. The anatomical driver joints were treated separately: limits were re-enabled on 14 hinges using the converted ranges. The rectus femoris route also lacked the patella tracking needed for knee-extension action; its intermediate path point was replaced by the vastus medialis patella-tracking point. The force comparison below quantifies the remaining differences at matched poses and activations. \paragraph{Contact and virtual geometry.} The converted model contained 19 explicit ground--body contact pairs, independent of geometry contact flags. Suspended tests remove these pairs; ground-contact tests retain only the six foot--ground pairs. Path-marker geometry is noncolliding. These settings isolate intended foot loading from unintended contact with other model components. These checks verify specific joint actions, coupled paths, and intended contacts. They retain the rigid-tendon approximation and do not establish full OpenSim equivalence or walking; the matched-pose force comparison below quantifies the remaining discrepancies. \subsection{Matched-Pose Force Comparison} \label{sec:muscle-models-compare} The comparison script reads OpenSim static-optimization forces and activations and assigns the corresponding poses and activations to the converted MuJoCo model. It calls the forward calculation at each pose; it does not assign the trajectory velocities or integrate muscle activation and fiber dynamics through time. OpenSim static optimization likewise solves frame-by-frame force allocation rather than a compliant-tendon forward simulation \citep{opensim_static_optimization_docs}. The comparison therefore tests force predictions along sampled poses at matched activation, not dynamic equivalence or tendon-energy storage. Table~\ref{tab:force-r2} retains the saved per-muscle statistics: $R^2=1-\mathrm{SSE}/\mathrm{SST}$, squared Pearson correlation, and the best-aligning lag in analysis frames. The calculation and saved values are in \nolinkurl{Code/MuJoCo_SNS/spinal/_muscle_force_compare.py} and \nolinkurl{muscle_force_compare.csv}. Eight of the twelve representative muscles have $R^2$ between 0.87 and 0.98 and mean-force ratios within \qty{10}{\percent}. Soleus and semimembranosus have lower agreement, and the negative $R^2$ values for rectus femoris and biceps femoris short head indicate errors larger than predicting the corresponding mean reference force. Shape correlation alone does not remove those errors. The rectus femoris path was altered during conversion, while the biceps femoris short-head result has a substantial force-scale difference. These observations identify muscles requiring closer inspection; they do not isolate routing, force-law, and velocity effects from one another. The frame-lag calculation describes alignment of these saved sequences. It cannot establish a physical delay caused by tendon compliance because the comparison does not simulate that mechanism. The results support selected pose-wise force checks, with the reported exceptions, rather than a general claim of equivalence for neural-control studies. A dynamic comparison would require matched velocities, activation histories, and explicitly specified tendon dynamics.

4–47, 74–76 Chapter~\ref{ch:sns} describes how I connected neural controllers to musculoskeletal models. Here I document the mechanical checks, signal definitions, and interventions needed to interpret their motion. A worked knee-reflex example in Section~\ref{sec:app_knee_transient} can be run from the two saved Simulink models. The subsequent experiments examine timing, loading, and mechanical assistance in the coupled walkers. Appendix~\ref{app:neuron_equations} gives the neuron equations and spiking calibration. \section{Mechanical-Model and Interface Verification}\label{sec:app_conversion_repairs} I first applied human muscle parameters to the robot's artificial-muscle routes. The routed lengths prevented the muscles from reaching equilibrium. I therefore used the converted human reference to develop controllers and checked its joint actions, muscle forces, and contacts against their source definitions. The rerouted robot and the repaired human model have different actuator geometry. \subsection{Conversion Repairs and Mechanical Checks} \paragraph{Joint directions and coordinate conventions.} Single-actuator tests exposed reversed hip-flexion and hip-adduction actions. I reversed the corresponding hinge axes to recover the OpenSim actions and kept the converted knee coordinate, in which flexion is negative. At each knee angle I also applied its prescribed translations. Rotating the knee while holding those coordinates fixed changes the muscle paths and invalidates the pose comparison. \paragraph{Small muscles and force scales.} I disabled quadratus femoris, gemelli, and piriformis bilaterally. Their six entries remain in the 92-actuator model with zero force. Eight other muscle entries had an active maximum-force scale of only \qty{1}{\newton}. I restored the four bilateral scales from the source model (Table~\ref{tab:app_force_scale_repairs}). \begin{table}[htbp] \centering \caption{Active maximum-force scales restored in the converted human model.} \label{tab:app_force_scale_repairs} \begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}} \toprule Muscle & Converted scale (\unit{\newton}) & Restored scale (\unit{\newton}) \\ \midrule Erector spinae & 1 & 2500 \\ Internal oblique & 1 & 900 \\ External oblique & 1 & 900 \\ Extensor hallucis longus & 1 & 162 \\ \bottomrule \end{tabular} \end{table} The passive-force scales already matched the source. The converted length ranges still differ, so the restored maximum forces alone cannot predict force agreement at a given pose. \paragraph{Patella-related paths and coupled joints.} The converted moving path points follow knee angle through coupled joints. Their sampled limits conflicted with the prescribed trajectories away from the sampled pose, restricting motion and producing large constraint forces. I removed the conflicting limits from the path-point joints, kept their couplings, and restored the converted ranges on 14 anatomical hinges. For rectus femoris I substituted the patella-tracking point of vastus medialis for the intermediate point that had failed to track the patella during knee extension. \paragraph{Contact and virtual geometry.} The converted model defined 19 explicit ground--body contact pairs independently of its geometry contact flags. I removed all 19 for suspended tests and retained the six foot--ground pairs for ground tests. Path-marker geometry was noncolliding. These choices determine which bodies can load a foot or supply external support during a trial. \subsection{Matched-Pose Force Comparison} \label{sec:muscle-models-compare} At each reference pose I assigned the OpenSim static-optimization activations and coordinates to the converted MuJoCo model and evaluated its muscle forces. OpenSim static optimization allocates forces frame by frame \citep{opensim_static_optimization_docs}. Neither the trajectory velocities nor a forward history of activation or fiber state entered this comparison. It measures agreement at matched posture and activation under the converted rigid-tendon approximation. Table~\ref{tab:force-r2} gives $R^2=1-\mathrm{SSE}/\mathrm{SST}$, squared Pearson correlation, and the lag in analysis frames that best aligns each pair of force sequences. The calculation and its per-muscle results are in \nolinkurl{Code/MuJoCo_SNS/spinal/_muscle_force_compare.py} and \nolinkurl{muscle_force_compare.csv}. Eight of twelve right-side muscles had $R^2$ between 0.87 and 0.98 and mean MuJoCo forces within \qty{10}{\percent} of OpenSim. Mean MuJoCo/OpenSim force ratios for the other four were 0.916 for soleus, 0.776 for semimembranosus, 1.088 for rectus femoris, and 1.846 for biceps femoris short head. The latter two had negative $R^2$, so predicting the mean reference force would give a smaller squared error. For biceps femoris short head, a squared shape correlation of 0.868 concealed its large force-scale error. The altered rectus femoris path is one candidate explanation for its discrepancy. Lag describes alignment of the sampled force sequences. Attributing a lag or scale error to a particular mechanism would require matched velocities, activation histories, and tendon dynamics. The comparison preserves the per-muscle statistics. The historical framewise MuJoCo forces are unavailable for a fresh calculation of every $R^2$.

84–115 \subsection{Neural and Actuator Signal Contracts}\label{sec:app_signal_contracts} The graphical and programmatic implementations require the same represented qua…
84–115 \subsection{Neural and Actuator Signal Contracts}\label{sec:app_signal_contracts} The graphical and programmatic implementations require the same represented quantities, units, signs, and update order at their interfaces. Neural membrane voltages, applied currents, and conductances use millivolts, nanoamperes, and microsiemens, respectively. The neuronal equations and spiking calibration are given in Appendix~\ref{app:neuron_equations}. The one-input SNS-Simscape synapse transmits the pair $[g_k,\,g_kE_k]^{\mathsf T}$ to its postsynaptic neuron, which evaluates \begin{equation} I_{\mathrm{syn}}=\sum_k g_kE_k-V\sum_k g_k =\sum_k g_k(E_k-V). \label{eq:app_synaptic_contract} \end{equation} Here $g_k$ is the conductance calculated from the presynaptic voltage and the specified saturation ramp, and $E_k$ is the reversal potential. Summing these two transmitted quantities within the neuron preserves the conductance-based driving force without a separate postsynaptic-voltage wire to every synapse. The \texttt{SynSum} block combines additional incoming pairs using the same operation. Motor output, actuator force, and sensory return remain separate interfaces. The biological-muscle block accepts dimensionless activation, musculotendon length in metres, and shortening velocity in metres per second. The characterized BPA blocks accept pressure in kilopascals and path length in metres and return tensile force in newtons. Their mask parameters specify resting, tendon, and fitting lengths. The quasi-static pressure--length force calculation does not supply valve-flow or pressure-transient dynamics. Conversely, the reduced knee demonstration uses its declared simplified actuator law and is not an additional validation of the characterized BPA equations. The MuJoCo--Simulink bridge exchanges command and measurement arrays between an existing MuJoCo mechanism and a Simulink controller. Each experiment must retain its array ordering, joint sign convention, command scaling, and timestep with its recording. This runtime interface does not translate arbitrary Simscape mechanisms into MuJoCo. The conversion records also identify machine-specific geometry paths and external-source patches that require preflight checking before a fresh MyoConverter run; the demonstrated repaired models are not evidence of a universal one-step conversion process. \section{Implementation and Circuit-Response Checks}\label{sec:sns_ports}\label{sec:crossengine_methods} Implementation agreement is assessed with a common circuit specification, identical input samples, and matched update settings. A separate coupled-model test is needed to establish what those neural outputs do to a mechanical model. This distinction also prevents a successful small circuit from conferring verification on a larger controller. \subsection{Matched Rhythm-Core Implementations} The contact-driven W2L comparison maps corresponding neuron parameters and shared rhythm pathways from the 95-neuron NumPy network into a representative 26-neuron Simulink core. Drive and prescribed heel inputs match. Over the post-startup \qtyrange{2}{12}{\second} interval, burst periods matched at \qty{1.000}{\second} and all ten expected bursts were reproduced. The correlation comparison uses 5,000 matching \qty{2}{\milli\second} samples through \qty{11.998}{\second}; best-lag correlations of the four RG traces were at least 0.99996 at a \qty{2}{\milli\second} relative lag (Figure~\ref{fig:w2l_crossengine}). Removing the phasic heel inputs caused latching in both implementations. The shared-signal agreement does not establish identical complete networks, autonomous oscillation, or sample-exact transfer. \begin{figure}[p] \centering \includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.55\textheight,keepaspectratio]{w2l_crossengine_20261116.png} \caption[Matched-input W2L rhythm-core comparison]{Corresponding W2L rhythm signals in the full NumPy network and representative Simulink core under identical drive and prescribed heel inputs. The reported correlation is a best-lag measure with \qty{2}{\milli\second} relative alignment; it is not unshifted sample agreement or a comparison of independent closed-loop mechanical trajectories.} \label{fig:w2l_crossengine} \end{figure} The separately translated Deng circuit produced a \qty{1.938}{\second} period against a \qty{1.94}{\second} NumPy reference, with antagonist correlation $r=-0.86$. Its archived AnimatLab configuration had an approximately \qty{0.221}{\second} period and an in-phase bilateral rhythm. The parameterizations differ, so antagonist alternation alone does not establish quantitative equivalence. A full 410-neuron export agreed with its NumPy reference to \qty{4.2e-06}{\milli\volt} over the early transient and subsequently diverged. A separate constant-drive realization approached a fixed point while its reference oscillated. These outcomes require configuration-specific verification before parameter transfer.
78–113 \subsection{Neural and Actuator Signal Contracts}\label{sec:app_signal_contracts} Membrane voltage, current, and conductance are represented in millivolts, nanoa…
78–113 \subsection{Neural and Actuator Signal Contracts}\label{sec:app_signal_contracts} Membrane voltage, current, and conductance are represented in millivolts, nanoamperes, and microsiemens in both the graphical and programmatic implementations. The one-input SNS-Simscape synapse passes the pair $[g_k,\,g_kE_k]^{\mathsf T}$ to the postsynaptic neuron, which calculates \begin{equation} I_{\mathrm{syn}}=\sum_k g_kE_k-V\sum_k g_k =\sum_k g_k(E_k-V). \label{eq:app_synaptic_contract} \end{equation} Here $g_k$ is the conductance calculated from the presynaptic voltage and the specified saturation ramp, and $E_k$ is the reversal potential. Summing these two transmitted quantities within the neuron preserves the conductance-based driving force without a separate postsynaptic-voltage wire to every synapse. The \texttt{SynSum} block combines additional incoming pairs using the same operation. The biological-muscle block takes dimensionless activation, musculotendon length in metres, and shortening velocity in metres per second. The characterized BPA blocks take pressure in kilopascals and path length in metres and return tensile force in newtons. Their mask parameters specify resting, tendon, and fitting lengths. The pressure--length law describes quasi-static force. Valve flow and pressure transients require separate models. The reduced knee and loaded-cup demonstrations each implement six connections. On each side, Ia excites its own MN and inhibits the antagonist MN, and Ib inhibits its own MN. The Ia block combines a position signal and signed velocity and clips its output to \qtyrange{0}{10}{\nano\ampere}. For knee angle $q$ and angular range $R$, the extensor velocity input is $\dot q/R$. The implemented flexor path applies a second gain of $-1/R$ to that signal, giving $-\dot q/R^2$. In the cup model, the two muscles receive opposite signed deviations and velocities, each divided by \qty{0.3}{\radian}. Both models feed tensile force directly to Ib. The knee's extensor and flexor gains are 0.0200 and \qty{0.0222}{\nano\ampere\per\newton}, respectively. Both cup gains are \qty{0.008}{\nano\ampere\per\newton}. The knee uses a simplified linear actuator. In the cup, filtered activation $A$ supplies $620A$\,kPa to each characterized BPA20 block. Neither circuit has an independent group II or foot-contact input. The graphical editor's Build action imports the saved graded neurons, synapses, and external current destinations into Simulink. In the saved W2L example, the left extensor PF projects to the left extensor MN, and the corresponding left flexor PF projects to the flexor MN. A user can inspect each connection's conductance, reversal potential, and activation ramp. The separate demonstration builders add mechanical equations, actuator blocks, and sensory wiring. The worked knee experiment below uses the coupled model generated by its demonstration builder. The MathWorks MuJoCo--Simulink C++ S-function exchanges command and measurement arrays with an existing MuJoCo mechanism. I adapted the build for MuJoCo 2.3.7 and Windows MinGW, selected keyframe 0 on reset when present, and added five right-side readbacks to an MJCF copy. They report knee angle and angular speed and vastus medialis path length, velocity, and force. The runtime interface preserves the mechanism supplied to it. Preparing that mechanism from OpenSim requires the corresponding geometry paths and MyoConverter patches. The E1 demonstration sends the right vastus medialis measurements through Ia, II, and Ib and returns a command to that actuator. Its length encoder computes $\operatorname{clip}\!\left(0.5+L/[2(L_{\max}-L_0)],0,1\right)$ from raw path length $L$, with $L_0=\qty{0.1734624224}{\meter}$ and $L_{\max}=\qty{0.24673}{\meter}$. The encoder reached 1 at all 300 noninitial \qty{5}{\milli\second} samples in each feedback-on and feedback-off recording. The II block therefore returned a constant \qty{4}{\nano\ampere}, and the length contribution to Ia stayed constant. The velocity and force signals could still vary. E1 demonstrates bidirectional command and measurement exchange. A length-evoked response would require an encoder with a changing output over the measured range. \section{Implementation and Circuit-Response Checks}\label{sec:sns_ports}\label{sec:crossengine_methods} I compared neural implementations with a common circuit definition, prescribed input samples, and matched update settings. Coupled experiments then tested the action of those outputs through a specified mechanical model. \subsection{Matched Rhythm-Core Implementations} I mapped the shared rhythm pathways of the 95-neuron NumPy W2L network into a 26-neuron Simulink core and prescribed the same drive and heel inputs. Both produced the ten expected bursts with a period of \qty{1.000}{\second} during $2\leq t<12$\,s. Across the 5,000 common samples at \qty{2}{\milli\second} spacing, ending at \qty{11.998}{\second}, the four RG traces had best-lag correlations of at least 0.99996 at a \qty{2}{\milli\second} relative lag (Figure~\ref{fig:w2l_crossengine}). Both circuits latched when I removed the phasic heel inputs. The shared core thus reproduces the driven rhythm with a small timing offset. \begin{figure}[p] \centering \includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.55\textheight,keepaspectratio]{w2l_crossengine_20261116.png} \caption[Matched-input W2L rhythm-core comparison]{Corresponding rhythm signals in the full NumPy W2L network and its Simulink core under a common drive and prescribed heel inputs. Best-lag correlation aligns the signals by \qty{2}{\milli\second}. Each implementation received the same external input sequence.} \label{fig:w2l_crossengine} \end{figure} The separately translated Deng circuit produced a \qty{1.938}{\second} period against a \qty{1.94}{\second} NumPy reference, with antagonist correlation $r=-0.86$. Its archived AnimatLab configuration had an approximately \qty{0.221}{\second} period and an in-phase bilateral rhythm. The parameterizations differ, so antagonist alternation alone does not establish quantitative equivalence. A full 410-neuron export agreed with its NumPy reference to \qty{4.2e-06}{\milli\volt} over the early transient and subsequently diverged. A separate constant-drive realization approached a fixed point while its reference oscillated. These outcomes require configuration-specific verification before parameter transfer.

118–131, 393, 448, 462 \subsection{Transient Knee Sensory-Current Response}\label{sec:app_knee_transient} The knee block model was tested from a settled operating point using a rectangular current pulse added after the spindle transducer and before the extensor Ia sensory-neuron membrane input. Stimulus and no-stimulus cases start from the same initial conditions and run continuously for \qty{50}{\second}; the \qty{2}{\nano\ampere} pulse acts from \qty{30.000}{\second} to \qty{30.030}{\second}. The original configuration did not settle: its knee angle spanned \ang{0.712} during the \qtyrange{25}{30}{\second} baseline window. Its former startup comparison is therefore not used to demonstrate transient reflex recovery. The graded-response comparison changes exactly six parameters: the two MN output ramp widths from 1 to \qty{10}{\milli\volt}, and the four Ia synaptic ramp widths from 0.5 to \qty{10}{\milli\volt}. Equations, thresholds, conductances, wiring, mechanics, tonic drives, and activation dynamics are retained. This reduces loop gain as an explicit engineering comparison, rather than fitting physiological parameters. The simple actuator-force law is distinct from the characterized BPA model of Chapter~\ref{ch:muscles}. The flexor activation is zero at baseline, so the experiment does not establish suppression of a tonically active antagonist. The modified configuration passes baseline settling tests for angle, velocity, all six membrane states, and both activations. Its flexion-negative knee baseline is \ang{-42.6392}; positive excursions indicate extension. Extension peaks at \ang{1.0908} after \qty{111}{\milli\second}. Recovery requires displacement within \ang{0.01} of baseline and absolute angular velocity below \qty{1e-4}{\radian\per\second}; both remain within those bounds from \qty{6.355}{\second} after stimulus onset. The late residual is approximately \ang{9.3e-7}. A fresh-process replay reproduces the resampled pulse arrays exactly. Halving the ODE45 maximum step from 1 to \qty{0.5}{\milli\second}, at unchanged tolerances, changes the angle trace by at most \ang{5.20e-5}. Comparisons use linear interpolation onto a \qty{1}{\milli\second} grid, without smoothing. These checks establish numerical recovery in the model, not physiological reflex fidelity. Figure~\ref{fig:sns_reflex_current} summarizes the stimulus, matched kinematics, and MN response. \subsection{A Published Neural Mechanism as a Verification Target}\label{sec:rybak_verification} The reduced Python implementation of the two-level circuit of \citet{rybak_modelling_2006} passed 13 checks covering drive-dependent activity, repeatability, and the distinction between resetting and non-resetting deletions. Stimulating the pattern-formation extensor population suppressed flexor motor output while the rhythm generator continued, so flexor activity resumed at its expected phase. Stimulating the rhythm-generator extensor population also suppressed flexor output but shifted the subsequent rhythm phase. This comparison tests the functional separation between rhythm generation and pattern formation: similar missing motor bursts can arise through different neural mechanisms. It is a neural-only verification of the translated circuit, independent of a mechanical model and separate from the Simulink demonstrations. The earlier \qty{5}{\second} knee startup comparison used a graded baseline and a spiking twin. Its final-window statistics describe startup activity; the settled, matched-control test in Section~\ref{sec:app_knee_transient} instead measures recovery from a brief sensory-current pulse. The earlier startup figure and its numerical table are retained with the supporting source records rather than repeated here. \path{demos/sns_build_demo.m}; \path{demos/sns_build_beer_demo.m} (S) & Executable reduced knee and loaded-cup mechanisms and their actual neural pathways. 115–149, 407, 461, 475 \subsection{Transient Knee Sensory-Current Response}\label{sec:app_knee_transient} In Section~\ref{sec:sns_simulink_demos}, I asked whether the single-joint knee would extend after a brief increase in extensor Ia current and return to its original posture (Figure~\ref{fig:sns_reflex_current}). In the original circuit, angle spanned \ang{0.712} during the intended baseline, $25\leq t<30$\,s. To obtain a settled posture I broadened the two MN output ramps from 1 to \qty{10}{\milli\volt} and the four Ia synaptic ramps from 0.5 to \qty{10}{\milli\volt}. These six changes reduce loop gain. The equations, thresholds, conductances, connections, tonic drive, activation dynamics, and single-joint mechanics are the same in the original and modified circuits. I ran a control and a stimulated trial continuously for \qty{50}{\second} from the same initial knee flexion of \qty{0.26}{\radian}, zero angular velocity and activation, and MN voltages of \qty{-52}{\milli\volt}. Tonic drive was \qty{4.2}{\nano\ampere} to the extensor MN and \qty{2.5}{\nano\ampere} to the flexor MN. Angle, speed, six membrane states, and two activations settled before stimulation. I report the negative of the model's internal flexion coordinate so that positive excursions indicate extension. The baseline was the mean control angle for $25\leq t<30$\,s, \ang{-42.6392}. The initial extension came from increased extensor force, which rose from \qty{7.37}{\newton} at pulse onset to \qty{25.94}{\newton} at pulse offset. The flexor MN output was zero at baseline, and flexor force was negligible during the pulse. Flexor force subsequently exceeded \qty{0.001}{\newton} at \qty{38}{\milli\second} after onset and reached \qty{14.4}{\newton} before maximum knee extension. The stimulated knee reached \ang{1.0908} more extension than the concurrent control \qty{111}{\milli\second} after pulse onset. ODE45 used relative tolerance $10^{-8}$ and absolute tolerance $10^{-10}$. Halving its maximum step from 1 to \qty{0.5}{\milli\second} changed the angle trace by at most \ang{5.20e-5} on the common, linearly interpolated \qty{1}{\milli\second} grid. Each control, pulse, and fine-step run contains 50,001 comparison times. \paragraph{Running the knee experiment.} From the repository root in a fresh MATLAB session with Simulink, run the commands below. They copy the saved library, knee model, and driver into a new temporary directory so that the driver can write its instrumented model and simulation results there. 135–184 \subsection{Layer-Specific Controls in the Reduced Deng Circuit}\label{sec:app_layer_stimulus} The fourteen-neuron Deng-derived implementation follows the circu…
135–184 \subsection{Layer-Specific Controls in the Reduced Deng Circuit}\label{sec:app_layer_stimulus} The fourteen-neuron Deng-derived implementation follows the circuit used in SNS-Toolbox demonstrations \citep{deng_neuromechanical_2019,nourse_sns_2023}. A \qty{0.1}{\milli\second} Euler step reproduced the existing neural integrator's complete \qty{24}{\second} baseline voltage trace exactly in the tested process. The baseline period was \qty{1.938}{\second}. Neural output drove the existing suspended, unilateral W2L mechanical model without afferent return. Hip commands came from the MN outputs; knee and ankle commands used the existing PF-to-activation mapping. This configuration tests transmitted circuit effects, not the original rat mechanics or bilateral walking. The initial sweep applied \qty{+2}{\nano\ampere} and \qty{+10}{\nano\ampere} to each layer for \qtylist{0.1;0.5}{\second}, together with two direct MN inhibitory controls. None met the deletion criterion. Subsequent stronger-current control series, including stimuli lasting two baseline periods (\qty{3.876}{\second}), retained the same circuit and mechanical parameters (Table~\ref{tab:layer_stimulus}). RG and PF stimuli began one-quarter cycle after a baseline RG onset; MN inhibition began \qty{0.05}{\second} into a baseline motor burst. Positive currents target the extensor RG or hip-PF population; negative currents target the hip-flexor MN. These currents are mechanistic controls, not experimentally calibrated physiological stimuli. A deletion required at least \qty{20}{\milli\second} of baseline motor activity in the stimulus window and suppression of at least half of that activity, using an activation threshold of 0.5. Phase residuals compare the final four recovered onsets with the nearest baseline onset, normalized by the baseline period. Residuals at or below 0.05 cycles were classified as non-resetting, and those at or above 0.15 as shifted; intermediate values remained indeterminate. RG and motor residuals were assessed separately. The two-cycle stimuli suppressed both expected flexor burst onsets at all three layers. Baseline and stimulated neural and mechanical trajectories were identical before stimulus onset. The \qty{1}{\second} PF pulse shifted the motor pattern without resetting the RG clock, whereas the two-cycle PF pulse preserved both schedules. The duration dependence cautions against treating every motor phase shift as an RG reset. The coupled mechanical traces contained large joint-limit violations, so these tests are interpreted through their neural responses in Table~\ref{tab:layer_stimulus}. \begin{table}[htbp] \centering\small \caption[Layer-specific stimulation controls]{Selected stronger-current controls in the reduced Deng-derived circuit. Phase residuals are fractions of the baseline cycle. ``No deletion'' means the specified suppression criterion was not met.} \label{tab:layer_stimulus} \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lrrcc>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}} \toprule Target & \unit{\nano\ampere} & \unit{\second} & RG phase & MN phase & Outcome \\ \midrule PF & $+50$ & 1.0 & 0.000 & 0.208 & Motor pattern shifted; RG preserved \\ RG & $+50$ & 3.876 & 0.211 & 0.211 & Resetting deletion \\ PF & $+50$ & 3.876 & 0.000 & 0.000 & Non-resetting deletion \\ MN & $-500$ & 3.876 & 0.000 & 0.000 & Non-resetting deletion \\ \bottomrule \end{tabularx} \end{table} \subsection{Archived AnimatLab Models}\label{sec:animatlab_validation} Headless reruns tested whether archived models reproduced their reference behavior in the original simulator. Eleven targetable rhythm and gait metrics passed their acceptance bands. The bilateral contact-driven model's rhythm generators had periods of \qtylist{0.450;0.467}{\second}; the modern W2L model showed joint oscillations at \qty{2.25}{\Hz}. The Li-model rerun reached \qty{0.637}{\meter\per\second} with step periods near \qty{1.3}{\second} \citep{li_bipedal_2017}. These are reference-model checks, not results for the combined Deng--Li model or evidence of unsupported walking. A separate MuJoCo translation of the Li controller and body did not reproduce that original-simulator walking result. In a \qty{20}{\second} ground test, mean forward speed was \qty{0.040}{\meter\per\second}, with eleven detected right-foot stance episodes and none on the left. The right-side period of \qty{1.294}{\second} alone therefore did not establish bilateral stepping. This failure distinguishes reproduction of the source model from successful transfer to another simulator. The original course-project circuit used a shared RG to supply bilateral PF and motor pathways. That source-model architecture for the motivating transfer attempt differs from the later W2L108 circuit, which has a separate RG on each side and additional sensory projections. The earlier instrumented AnimatLab trial records joint, neural, sensory, and body-motion channels over \qty{10}{\second} at a \qty{0.2}{\milli\second} interval. Figure~\ref{fig:animatlab_short_response} retains an aligned short window. Contact-sensory channels are membrane voltages, not contact forces. The initial external horizontal force and absence of a recorded timed vertical release prevent interpreting the run as an independent drop, touchdown, or stable-walking test. The reorganized bilateral project is a different configuration; its completion of simulation runs without sustained alternation is an assembly result. \begin{figure}[p] \centering \includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{animatlab_short_response_20261116.png} \caption[Archived AnimatLab neural and mechanical recording]{Aligned RG, PF, and MN activity and hip, knee, and ankle motion from the same suspended AnimatLab trial over \qtyrange{1}{2.5}{\second}. Extensor and flexor pathways are blue solid and orange dashed. Voltages are graded membrane signals, and the original joint conventions, including positive knee flexion, are retained. This is the earlier instrumented configuration.} \label{fig:animatlab_short_response} \end{figure} \section{Staged Calibration and Coupled-Model Experiments}\label{sec:sns_tuning_methods}\label{sec:sns_curriculum} Neural calibration and the physical curriculum test different requirements. Ordered neural fits evaluate intrinsic rhythm, timing with expected afferents, feedforward motor recruitment, and local-feedback refinement. Their activation targets are inverse-mechanics estimates from the reference motion, not measured electromyograms. A successful output fit is therefore followed by tests in the actual mechanical model. Each selected configuration retains its source hashes, settings, trial records, matched controls, and independent-process repeat. The physical curriculum separates deafferented air motion, afferented air motion, standing, supported stepping, and contact refinement; completing one stage does not establish the later stages.

184–210 Neural calibration and the physical curriculum test different requirements. Ordered neural fits evaluate intrinsic rhythm, timing with expected afferents, feedfo…
184–210 Neural calibration and the physical curriculum test different requirements. Ordered neural fits evaluate intrinsic rhythm, timing with expected afferents, feedforward motor recruitment, and local-feedback refinement. Their activation targets are inverse-mechanics estimates from the reference motion, not measured electromyograms. A successful output fit is therefore followed by tests in the actual mechanical model. Each selected configuration retains its source hashes, settings, trial records, matched controls, and independent-process repeat. The physical curriculum separates deafferented air motion, afferented air motion, standing, supported stepping, and contact refinement; completing one stage does not establish the later stages. The acceptance procedure first checks finite states, completion of the specified duration, anatomical knee bounds, and the required movement. Cadence, bilateral phase, loading, clearance, and assistance are then interpreted for that experiment. The historical gait score's reserved no-cycle value is $-25$; it must be rejected before ranking, since its numerical value exceeds many genuine movement scores. Scores from different configurations are not a common measure of locomotor quality. \subsection{BiW2Lvar+ Air Stages}\label{sec:app_biw2l_air} The revised graded BiW2Lvar+ controller completed both air stages with the same learned weights and a shared PF-to-MN expression scale of 0.1. The archived selector \texttt{hunt} is a compatibility identifier for this implementation; Hunt's contribution is the calibration method, not authorship of the controller. Table~\ref{tab:app_air_stages} reports the principal-cycle audit. The \qty{16}{\second} runs use the native MuJoCo biological-muscle formulation and the repaired Gait2392 human model. The retained adapter defines stage 1 with sensory return disabled and sustained normalized flexor and extensor commands set to zero. It defines stage 2 with live sensory return enabled and each command set to 0.15 on both sides, consistent with the fitted command manifest. That adapter also defines the same initialization seed for both stages, an added 0.4 on the right extensor and left flexor command channels for the first 50 neural calls (\qty{100}{\milli\second}); this seed does not impose a continuing stride clock. It clamps the postural, balance, and functional-command channels off, sets direct tonic RG current to zero, and disables the legacy shared-drive input. The original launch adapter and complete consumed-command traces were not recovered; these are declared adapter settings rather than independently verified input samples from that launch. Thus these air stages cannot be treated as a matched feedback-only comparison. The retained shared descending drive in the separate historical weaning study below belongs to a different configuration. \begin{table}[htbp] \centering\small \caption[Fixed-controller air-stage evaluations]{BiW2Lvar+ air stages using unchanged learned weights. Negative knee angles indicate flexion. Sensory return and sustained commands both differ between stages.} \label{tab:app_air_stages} \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}Xrrrr@{}} \toprule Condition & Cycles R/L & Period (s) & Phase error & \shortstack{Maximum knee angle\\R/L (deg)} \\ \midrule Deafferented air & 4/4 & 2.466 & 0.0337 & $-1.300/-1.326$ \\ Afferented air & 7/7 & 1.300 & 0.0933 & $-1.718/-1.801$ \\ \bottomrule \end{tabularx} \end{table} The original stage-1 counter counted secondary flexion humps as additional cycles. The preserved principal-cycle audit instead uses autocorrelation and separated principal peaks; its checks accept antiphase double-hump signals and reject synchronous, frozen, and fast test signals. The legacy failed assessment remains in the record. Stage 2 passes both counters. Secondary humps remain a waveform-quality limitation, even when the principal-motion criterion passes. Ground contact is disabled and measured foot forces are zero. The adopted stage-2 suspension statistic is \qty{99.860}{\percent}, calculated from \qty{768.662}{\newton} mean spring force over \qtyrange{5}{16}{\second} using the legacy total model mass of \qty{78.4646}{\kilogram} and weight of \qty{769.737726}{\newton}. That mass includes a separate fixed \qty{0.1}{\kilogram} ground body. The aligned-recording metadata instead uses the pelvis subtree, \qty{78.3646}{\kilogram} or \qty{768.756726}{\newton}; dividing the same legacy force samples by that denominator gives \qty{99.9877}{\percent}. The adopted percentage is retained with its original denominator. Figure~\ref{fig:sns_biw2l_air} displays unnormalized force in newtons. The legacy samples are tagged at interval start, while aligned states are tagged at interval end, so an independently selected aligned window must also retain its own timestamps. These measures characterize suspension, with frontal guidance retained, rather than a minimum ground-walking support requirement. Higher-expression trials stopped at the predictive knee-extension guard; the completed condition retains the guard and does not clip joint state. This controller has not completed standing or ground stages.

These settings describe the compatible retained implementation. In that adapter, group Ia receives signed path velocity divided by \qty{0.6}{\meter\per\second}, and group II receives signed path-length deviation from its fitted midpoint divided by its fitted half-range. Both normalized inputs are clipped to $[-1,1]$ before current gain is applied. For each muscle, group Ib receives positive tension $\max(-F_{\rm actuator},0)$ divided by its maximum force and multiplied by the Ib gain. The central load input separately scales summed ipsilateral antigravity-muscle tension by its reference target force. These are modeled musculotendon signals.
